snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Causal interpretation improves when the model states what evidence would weaken

the

proposed

mechanism.

In

models

of

unintended

effects,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

A defensible account requires the exposure, mediator, and outcome to be distinguished

empirically.

A

study

of

unintended

effects

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative record is not evidence of absence, but neither is incomplete measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

21.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in payment precede variation in the relevant health

outcome?

•

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected

to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

•

Interpretation here depends on separating the observed endpoint from the pathway

that

produced

it.

•

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected

to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

•

For metrics, which populations or settings show the longest delay between material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning coding would falsify, reverse, or materially narrow the

proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

21.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on incentives is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

22. The Normalization of Being Ill

When chronic illness becomes common, societies can adapt around disease rather

than

remove

upstream

causes.

Adaptation

is

often

necessary

and

humane,

but

it

can

coexist

with

prevention

failure.

SR

asks

when

normalization

represents

inclusion

and

when

it

becomes

institutional

accommodation

to

avoidable

exposure.

22.1 Prevalence Prevalence cannot be inferred from the domain label alone. For prevalence, causal

resolution

depends

on

observing

the

proposed

source

and

mediator

rather

than

inferring

both

from

the

final

diagnosis.

Operational

independence

keeps

the

field

from

treating

the

existence

of

an

institution

or

environment

as

proof

of

its

biological

effect.

The endpoint can be clinically clear while its upstream architecture remains uncertain.

For

prevalence,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

A credible estimate must account for both the accumulation of load and the possibility

of

recovery.

In

models

of

prevalence,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

At this stage, measurement has to distinguish the visible outcome from the underlying

process.

A

study

of

prevalence

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

22.2 Adaptation The evidentiary problem posed by adaptation is concrete. The field treats adaptation

as

a

pathway

only

when

the

exposure

can

be

located,

its

recipients

identified,

its

mechanism

defended,

and

its

downstream

signal

specified.

This

structure turns a broad SR proposition into a sequence of claims that can be estimated,

challenged,

and

reproduced.

A defensible account requires the exposure, mediator, and outcome to be distinguished

empirically.

For

adaptation,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

observation

must

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

The analysis should therefore follow the pathway across exposure, embodiment,

detection,

and

response.

In

of

action

a

defensive

footnote.

The relevant comparison is between plausible causal architectures, not between

structure

and

individual

agency.

A

study

of

adaptation

should

document which variables are directly observed, which are proxies, what is missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

record

is

evidence

an

22.9 Chapter Conclusion

The SR position on normalization is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

23. Housing, Labor, Income, and Recovery Capacity Recovery requires time, safety, sleep, nutrition, and access to care. These resources

are

unequally

distributed.

Infrastructural

Medicine

therefore

treats

recovery

capacity

as

a

population

variable

shaped

by

housing,

labor,

income,

paid

leave,

transportation,

and

caregiving

arrangements.

23.1 Housing Quality A useful analysis of housing quality starts by locating the condition in an observable

chain.

The

causal

chain

for

housing

quality

must

be

explicit

enough

to

show

where

the

pressure

originates,

who

bears

it,

how

it

is

embodied,

and

what

measurable

consequence

follows.

This

separation

is

methodologically important because structural plausibility is not itself evidence

of

mediation.

The pathway should be evaluated against both its predicted signal and its plausible

rivals.

For

housing

quality,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

observation

must

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

the

proposed

mechanism.

models

housing

quality,

action

may

mediate

upstream

the

downstream

trajectory.

study

of

housing

23.4 Leave What matters for leave is not its rhetorical plausibility but its measurable position

in

the

pathway.

For

leave,

the

identifies

from

exposure,

and

collapse

changes

the

trajectory.

investigators

distinguish

23.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in housing quality precede variation in the relevant

health

outcome?

•

This distinction matters because administrative records and causal processes do

not

necessarily

align.

•

A longitudinal view is essential because the relevant load may precede diagnosis

by

years.

•

A stronger analysis begins by isolating the mechanism at issue. •

For income, which populations or settings show the longest delay between material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning leave would falsify, reverse, or materially narrow the

proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

23.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on recovery infrastructure is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

24. Children and Developmental Infrastructure Children are uniquely important because developmental timing changes the meaning

of

exposure.

The

same

stressor

can

have

different

consequences

depending

on

age,

duration,

buffering

relationships,

and

developmental

stage.

The

field

requires

heightened

caution

against

deterministic

claims

while

recognizing

that

early

conditions

can

alter

trajectories.

24.1 Prenatal Exposure

For prenatal exposure, the causal claim begins with definition rather than interpretation.

A

study

of

prenatal

exposure

should

trace

the

condition

from

its

upstream

source

through

exposure

and

mediation

to

an

outcome

that

can

be

observed

independently.

Measurement

at

each

link

also

makes

competing

explanations

easier

to

compare

and

replication

easier

to

perform.

A stronger analysis begins by isolating the mechanism at issue. For prenatal exposure,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Mechanistic plausibility is only the beginning; the proposed sequence must also

fit

observed

timing.

In

models

of

prenatal

exposure,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection, reverse causation, and competing mechanisms therefore belong in the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

A useful design must preserve heterogeneity instead of treating variation as noise.

A

study

of

prenatal

exposure

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

record

is

not

evidence

absence,

incomplete

25. The Desirable Patient and Institutional Legibility Systems tend to process some patients more easily than others. Patients with stable

housing,

time,

transportation,

documentation,

digital

access,

health

and

predictable

symptoms

are

easier

to

route

through

standardized

pathways.

SR

studies

whether

institutional

design

systematically

penalizes

complexity

and

then

misreads

navigation

failure

as

patient

failure.

25.2 Documentation

For documentation, the causal claim begins with definition rather than interpretation.

For

documentation,

the

analysis

identifies

the

upstream

determinant,

the

exposed

population,

the

plausible

mediator,

and

the

downstream

outcome

that

should

change

if

the

proposed

pathway

is

real.

Independent

operationalization

is

necessary

because

a

convincing

narrative

can

otherwise

conceal

a

weak

or

missing

causal

link.

The empirical burden is therefore to identify what changed, when it changed, and

through

which

mechanism.

For

documentation,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

What matters here is not the existence of correlation alone but the structure that

would

generate

it.

In

of

documentation,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

Confounding,

rather

footnote.

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected to

individual-level

25.7 SR Formalization Risk(t) = f[Exposure(t), Susceptibility, Duration, Recovery, Care Access] SlowHarm(t) = ∫ ₀ ᵗ max(0, Load( τ) − Recovery( τ)) d τ VisibilityLag = T(recognition) − T(material change) The formal expressions in this chapter are conceptual measurement scaffolds rather

than

established

clinical

calculators.

They

state

what

would

need

to

be

measured

if

the

pathway

is

to

Disease-specific

parameters,

25.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in compliance precede variation in the relevant health

outcome?

•

The claim becomes testable once exposure history and recovery conditions are

represented

separately.

•

The next analytical step is to determine whether the observed pattern survives

alternative

explanations.

•

The empirical burden is therefore to identify what changed, when it changed,

and

through

which

mechanism.

•

For communication, which populations or settings show the longest delay between

material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning time would falsify, reverse, or materially narrow the proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

25.9 Chapter Conclusion

The SR position on patient legibility is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

26. AI, Digital Health, and Diagnostic Infrastructure Digital systems can improve detection, coordination, and access, but they can also

amplify

coding

errors,

measurement

bias,

fragmented

records,

and

automation

dependence.

Infrastructural

Medicine

treats

AI

as

part

of

the

diagnostic

substrate:

neither

autonomous

savior

nor

intrinsic

threat,

but

an

infrastructure

whose

effects

depend

on

data,

governance,

workflow,

and

feedback.

26.1 Algorithms The chapter treats algorithms as a candidate mechanism, not as a conclusion. For

algorithms,

causal

depends

on

observing

the

proposed

source

and

mediator

rather

than

inferring

both

from

the

final

quantities should enter the analysis independently, allowing one part of the pathway

to

be

the

both

direction

and

magnitude.

observed

timing.

align.

which

Automation cannot be inferred from the domain label alone. A defensible automation

connects

a

upstream

a

exposed

a

of

that

can

be

estimated,

challenged,

and

reproduced.

by

both

and

it,

the

mechanism

through

which

it

could

matter,

and

the

outcome

expected

to

respond.

This

separation

is

methodologically

important

because

structural

plausibility

is

not

itself

evidence

of

mediation.

The endpoint can be clinically clear while its upstream architecture remains uncertain.

For

visibility,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions are relevant. The observation window must also include plausible latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Mechanistic plausibility is only the beginning; the proposed sequence must also

fit

observed

timing.

In

models

of

visibility,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The pathway should be evaluated against both its predicted signal and its plausible

rivals.

A

study

of

visibility

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

26.7 SR Formalization

Risk(t) = f[Exposure(t), Susceptibility, Duration, Recovery, Care Access] SlowHarm(t) = ∫ ₀ ᵗ max(0, Load( τ) − Recovery( τ)) d τ VisibilityLag = T(recognition) − T(material change) These SR expressions are conceptual measurement scaffolds rather than established

clinical

calculators.

They

state

what

would

need

to

be

measured

if

the

proposed

pathway

is

to

become

an

empirical

model.

Disease-specific

parameters,

compatible

scales,

validation

samples,

uncertainty

intervals,

and

sensitivity

analyses

are

required

before

numerical

interpretation.

26.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in algorithms precede variation in the relevant health

outcome?

•

The model gains precision by separating what the system records from what the

subject

actually

experiences.

•

For causal purposes, the decisive issue is how the proposed pathway behaves

over

time.

•

This question cannot be resolved from prevalence alone; it requires pathway-specific

evidence.

•

For decision support, which populations or settings show the longest delay between

material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning bias would falsify, reverse, or materially narrow the proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

26.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on digital health is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

27. Measurement Architecture The field becomes scientific only when its concepts can be operationalized. Measurement

must

connect

upstream

variables

to

intermediate

exposures,

biological

markers,

clinical

outcomes,

and

institutional

response.

Composite

SR

indices

may

summarize

patterns,

but

they

cannot

substitute

for

validation

against

observable

outcomes.

27.1 Exposure Indices Before exposure indices can enter an SR model, its causal role has to be delimited.

For

exposure

indices,

causal

resolution

depends

on

observing

the

proposed

source

and

mediator

rather

than

inferring

both

from

the

final

diagnosis.

No

single

composite

label

should

substitute

for

direct

evidence

about

the

source,

exposure,

mediator,

and

endpoint.

The evidence becomes more informative when this relationship is specified directly.

For

exposure

indices,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

What matters here is not the existence of correlation alone but the structure that

would

generate

it.

In

models

of

exposure

indices,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection, reverse causation, and competing mechanisms therefore belong in the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

Interpretation here depends on separating the observed endpoint from the pathway

that

produced

it.

A

study

of

exposure

indices

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

27.2 Biomarkers The chapter treats biomarkers as a candidate mechanism, not as a conclusion. Research

on

biomarkers

should

distinguish

the

condition

producing

the

exposure

from

the

people

receiving

it,

the

mechanism

carrying

it

forward,

and

the

endpoint

used

to

test

the

claim.

Operational

independence

keeps

the

field

from

treating

the

existence

of

an

institution

or

environment

as

proof

of

its

biological

effect.

At this stage, measurement has to distinguish the visible outcome from the underlying

process.

For

biomarkers,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated, intermittent, cumulative, and developmentally timed exposure where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

The endpoint can be clinically clear while its upstream architecture remains uncertain.

In

models

of

biomarkers,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The analysis is strengthened when competing pathways remain visible rather than

being

compressed

into

one

explanation.

A

study

of

biomarkers

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete measurement evidence that an unobserved structural mechanism must

exist.

27.3 Time Series Time Series is the point at which the chapter becomes specific. The time series

pathway

is

testable

only

when

its

source,

distribution,

mediator,

and

predicted

health

consequence

are

specified

separately.

This

separation

is

methodologically

important

because

structural

plausibility

is

not

itself

evidence

of

mediation.

The pathway should be evaluated against both its predicted signal and its plausible

rivals.

For

time

series,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

This relationship is best evaluated as a sequence rather than as a static association.

In

models

of

time

series,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share causes with the outcome. Biological predisposition and protective factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The next analytical step is to determine whether the observed pattern survives alternative

explanations.

A

study

of

time

series

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

mechanism

must

27.4 Causal Graphs For causal graphs, the causal claim begins with definition rather than interpretation.

The

field

treats

causal

graphs

as

a

pathway

only

when

the

exposure

can

be

located,

its

recipients

identified,

its

mechanism

defended,

and

its

downstream

signal

specified.

No

single

composite

label

should

substitute

for

direct

evidence

about

the

source,

exposure,

mediator,

and

endpoint.

What matters here is not the existence of correlation alone but the structure that

would

generate

it.

For

causal

graphs,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Causal interpretation improves when the model states what evidence would weaken

the

proposed

mechanism.

In

models

of

causal

graphs,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

This part of the argument turns on temporal ordering and measurable mediation.

A

study

of

causal

graphs

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is not evidence of absence, but neither is incomplete measurement evidence that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

27.5 Natural Experiments A useful analysis of natural experiments starts by locating the condition in an observable

chain.

Research

on

natural

experiments

should

distinguish

the

condition

producing

the

exposure

from

the

people

receiving

it,

the

mechanism

carrying

it

forward,

and

the

endpoint

used

to

test

the

claim.

Keeping

those

components

separate

prevents

the

infrastructure

label

from

doing

explanatory

work

that

belongs

to

measurement.

Interpretation here depends on separating the observed endpoint from the pathway

that

produced

it.

For

natural

experiments,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

The

observation

window

latency

and

otherwise

can

misrepresent

both

For causal purposes, the decisive issue is how the proposed pathway behaves over

In

models

of

natural

experiments,

individual

action

may

mediate

an upstream condition, modify susceptibility, respond to early symptoms, or share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

A useful design must preserve heterogeneity instead of treating variation as noise.

A

study

of

natural

experiments

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

an

27.6 Uncertainty Uncertainty cannot be inferred from the domain label alone. The causal chain for

uncertainty

must

be

explicit

enough

to

show

where

the

pressure

originates,

who

bears

it,

how

it

is

embodied,

and

what

measurable

consequence

follows.

This

separation

is

methodologically

important

because

structural

plausibility

is

not

itself

evidence

of

mediation.

The relevant causal question changes once timing and exposure history are made

explicit.

For

uncertainty,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

The observed outcome should be read alongside the conditions that made it more

or

less

likely.

In

models

of

uncertainty,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The next analytical step is to determine whether the observed pattern survives alternative

explanations.

A

study

of

uncertainty

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative record is not evidence of absence, but neither is incomplete measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

clinical

calculators.

They

state

what

would

to

if

to

Disease-specific

27.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in exposure indices precede variation in the relevant

health

outcome?

•

What matters here is not the existence of correlation alone but the structure that

would

generate

it.

•

The pathway should be evaluated against both its predicted signal and its plausible

rivals.

•

For causal purposes, the decisive issue is how the proposed pathway behaves

over

time.

•

material

and

•

27.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on measurement is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

28. From Structural Theory to Causal Identification

Infrastructural Medicine becomes scientifically useful only when its structural

claims

can

be

translated

into

estimands

that

distinguish

exposure,

embodiment,

outcome,

and

alternative

explanations.

The

equations

used

elsewhere

in

this

manuscript

therefore

operate

at

three

different

levels:

theoretical

operators,

measurement

models,

and

causal

estimators.

Keeping

those

levels

separate

prevents

a

conceptual

identity

from

being

mistaken

for

an

estimated

biological

law.

28.1 Level I — Theoretical Operators A theoretical operator states the architecture that SR proposes. Slow Harm, for

example,

is

represented

as

unresolved

load

accumulated

through

time:

SHᵢ(t) = ∫ ₀ ᵗ max[0, Lᵢ( τ) − Rᵢ( τ)] d τ This expression defines a hypothesis about accumulation. It does not establish

that

load

and

recovery

have

already

been

measured

on

a

common

clinical

scale,

that

the

relationship

is

linear,

or

that

every

disease

responds

to

accumulated

load

in

the

same

way.

Those

are

empirical

questions.

28.2 Level II — Measurement Models

Latent SR constructs must be operationalized from observable indicators rather

than

assigned

arbitrary

weights.

A

generic

measurement

model

for

Slow

Harm

can

be

written:

xᵢ = Λ SHᵢ* + ε ᵢ where xᵢ is a vector of observed indicators, Λ is a vector or matrix of empirically

estimated

loadings,

SHᵢ*

is

the

latent

Slow

Harm

construct,

and

ε ᵢ

is

measurement

error.

The

Eroded

Subject

can

be

handled

analogously:

zᵢ = Θ ESᵢ* + ν ᵢ Confirmatory factor analysis, structural equation modeling, item-response methods,

reliability

testing,

measurement

invariance,

and

out-of-sample

validation

are

possible

tools.

The

choice

depends

on

the

data.

The

manuscript

does

not

assume

that

a

single

universal

Eroded

Subject

score

already

exists.

28.3 Level III — Causal Estimands For a binary infrastructural exposure X, the average treatment effect is: ATE = E[Y(1) − Y(0)] For two levels x and x′ of a continuous or policy exposure: τ( x,x′) = E[Y(x) − Y(x′)]

These potential-outcome expressions clarify the empirical question: what would

the

outcome

have

been

under

a

different

exposure

state

for

otherwise

comparable

units?

In

observational

health

data

the

counterfactual

is

not

observed,

so

identification

requires

assumptions

and

design

rather

than

algebra

alone.

28.4 Confounding, Reverse Causation, and Selection The minimum causal graph for an infrastructural claim contains exposure X, outcome

Y,

measured

confounders

C,

and

potentially

unmeasured

causes

U.

A

critic

can

correctly

reject

a

naive

association

if

common

causes

explain

both

exposure

and

outcome.

C → X C → Y X → Y Reverse causation is equally important in health. Illness can reduce income, destabilize

employment,

alter

diet,

increase

housing

insecurity,

and

generate

administrative

burden.

Longitudinal

ordering

must

therefore

distinguish:

X  → Y  ₊₁

from: Y  → X  ₊₁ Where feasible, the empirical program should use lagged exposure, individual

or

geographic

fixed

effects,

natural

experiments,

interrupted

time

series,

difference-in-differences,

regression

discontinuity,

instrumental

variables

with

defensible

exclusion

restrictions,

negative

controls,

and

sensitivity

analysis.

No

method

is

automatically

causal;

each

design

must

defend

its

own

assumptions.

28.5 Mediation as a Direct Test of the SR Mechanism The Eroded Subject architecture predicts a mechanism rather than a simple exposure–outcome

correlation:

X → SH → ES → Y A mediation analysis should therefore estimate the total effect of X on Y and decompose

the

pathway

only

under

explicit

identification

assumptions.

In

simple

notation:

TE = DE + IE

where TE is the total effect, DE the direct component not transmitted through the

modeled

mediator,

and

IE

the

indirect

component.

In

nonlinear

or

interacting

systems

the

decomposition

can

require

counterfactual

natural

or

interventional

effects

rather

than

this

simple

additive

identity.

The

important

scientific

point

is

that

SR

should

be

weakened

if

the

proposed

mediators

fail

repeatedly

to

carry

the

effect.

28.6 Interaction and Heterogeneous Effects Infrastructural Medicine does not predict uniform outcomes. Susceptibility, age,

sex,

developmental

stage,

comorbidity,

genetics,

baseline

disease,

social

support,

and

treatment

can

modify

the

effect

of

the

same

exposure.

A

basic

interaction

model

is:

Y = β ₀ + β ₁ X + β ₂ S + β ₃ ( X×S) + γ ᵀC + ε The coefficient β ₃ tests whether the association or identified effect of X differs

across

susceptibility

S.

Heterogeneity

is

therefore

a

prediction

to

investigate,

not

evidence

that

infrastructure

is

irrelevant.

28.7 Falsification Conditions

A structural pathway should be rejected, narrowed, or reformulated when its required

empirical

implications

fail.

Evidence

against

an

SR

pathway

includes:

no

reproducible

exposure–outcome

relationship

under

adequate

measurement

and

power;

incorrect

temporal

ordering;

failure

of

the

proposed

mediator;

disappearance

of

the

effect

under

credible

confounder

control;

a

natural

experiment

that

changes

the

proposed

cause

without

changing

the

predicted

downstream

variables;

or

a

rival

model

that

explains

the

data

materially

better.

This section therefore converts the mathematics from illustrative notation into a

research

contract.

SR

specifies

what

it

expects

to

observe

and

what

would

count

against

it.

29. First Empirical Test — Waiting-Time Slow Harm in Elective Hip and Knee Arthroplasty

Waiting for medically indicated care is not empty time. It is an exposure interval

during

which

the

underlying

disease,

pain,

disability,

uncertainty,

medication

burden,

work

loss,

caregiving

demand,

and

psychological

strain

may

continue.

Infrastructural

Medicine

therefore

treats

delayed

access

as

a

candidate form of Slow Harm when the delay exceeds a clinically meaningful interval

and

produces

measurable

deterioration.

29.1 Defining the Exposure Wᵢ = T ᵣₑₐ  ₑ  ,ᵢ − T_dₑcᵢ ᵢₒ ,ᵢ Wᵢ is the observed wait from the defined clinical decision point to treatment. Where

a

validated

clinical

or

system

benchmark

Bᵢ

exists,

excess

waiting

is:

EWTᵢ = max(0, Wᵢ − Bᵢ) The benchmark must be procedure- and priority-specific. A 26-week benchmark

for

elective

joint

replacement

cannot

be

transferred

to

radiation

therapy,

cancer

surgery,

hip

fracture

repair,

or

diagnostic

imaging.

29.2 Waiting-Time Slow Harm A conceptual accumulation operator is: SHᵂᵢ = ∫  ₀ ᵗ¹ qᵢ( τ) · 𝟙 [Wᵢ( τ) > Bᵢ] d τ qᵢ( τ) is the empirically measured burden rate during excess waiting: pain, functional

impairment,

disease

progression,

psychological

deterioration,

treatment

escalation,

emergency

utilization,

lost

work,

or

another

outcome

appropriate

to

the

condition.

This

formulation

is

deliberately

outcome-specific; it does not assume that every day of waiting produces identical

harm.

29.3 Canadian System Signal Canadian Institute for Health Information data show that timely access remains

incomplete

even

as

procedure

volumes

have

risen.

In

2024,

68%

of

hip

replacements

and

61%

of

knee

replacements

were

completed

within

the

26-week

benchmark;

69%

of

cataract

procedures

were

completed

within

the

112-day

benchmark.

Radiation

therapy

within

28

days

was

94%,

and

hip

fracture

repair

within

48

hours

was

83%.

Median

waits

for

MRI

were

15

days

longer

in

2024

than

in

2019,

and

median

waits

for

several

cancer

surgeries

also

increased

(Canadian

Institute

for

Health

Information

[CIHI],

2025).

These figures establish the existence and distribution of waiting exposure. They

do

not

by

themselves

prove

that

a

particular

delay

caused

a

particular

patient's

deterioration.

29.4 Quantitative Test and Results: A Narrow Arthroplasty Domain This first empirical test is intentionally narrow. It concerns adults already selected

for

primary

elective

total

hip

or

total

knee

arthroplasty.

It

does

not

test all surgical waiting, all healthcare waiting, cancer progression, cardiac-event

risk,

diagnostic

delay,

psychiatric

waiting,

mortality

attributable

to

delay,

or

the

full

Infrastructural

Medicine

field.

Its

purpose

is

to

determine

whether

one

specified

Slow

Harm

prediction

survives

contact

with

quantitative

evidence:

longer

presurgical

waiting

should

be

associated

with

greater

measurable

burden

during

the

waiting

interval.

The strongest quantitative synthesis currently available is Cooper, Bayram, and

Clement

(2024),

a

systematic

review

of

26

studies

with

16

studies

entering

meta-analysis

and

89,996

patients

overall.

Linear

meta-regression

identified

deterioration

in

joint

function

of

0.0575

points

on

a

100-point

scale

per

additional

day

of

waiting

(95%

CI

0.0064

to

0.1086;

p

=

.028;

I²

=

73.1%).

The

confidence

interval

excludes

zero.

For

this

prespecified

outcome,

the

null

hypothesis

of

no

waiting-time

relationship

is

rejected

in

the

published

meta-regression.

The same review found deterioration in health-related quality of life with additional

waiting

time

(reported

meta-regression

p

=

.011),

although

heterogeneity

was

high.

High

heterogeneity

matters:

it

indicates

that

the

magnitude of deterioration is not uniform across populations, instruments, and

waiting

environments.

Infrastructural

Medicine

therefore

interprets

the

result

as

evidence

for

a

bounded

exposure

effect,

not

a

universal

daily

damage

constant.

Canadian prospective evidence points in the same direction. Desmeules et al. (2010)

followed

153

patients

awaiting

knee

replacement

in

Québec

City.

Mean

presurgical

waiting

was

183

days

(SD

121.9).

Patients

waiting

more

than

9

to

12

months

deteriorated

in

WOMAC

pain

by

9.9

points

(95%

CI

0.54

to

19.2

in

deterioration

magnitude),

WOMAC

function

by

11.1

points

(95%

CI

3.4

to

18.7),

and

SF-36

physical

functioning

by

11.3

points

(95%

CI

4.2

to

18.4).

Longer-wait

groups

also

showed

deterioration

in

contralateral-knee

pain.

These

findings

establish

that

the

waiting

interval

can

contain

clinically

meaningful

burden

rather

than

functioning

as

administratively

empty

time.

Mahon et al. (2002), studying elective total hip arthroplasty in London, Ontario,

supplies

an

important

boundary.

Longer

waiting

was

not

associated

with

poorer

postoperative

health-related

quality

of

life

or

mobility.

Cooper

et

al. (2024) likewise did not establish a pooled relationship between presurgical waiting

duration

and

postoperative

outcome.

The

first

test

therefore

does

not

support

the

stronger

proposition

that

longer

arthroplasty

waits

necessarily

create

permanent

postoperative

damage.

The empirical result is consequently asymmetric but informative: the evidence

supports

accumulation

of

preoperative

burden

while

patients

wait,

yet

does

not

establish

irreversibility

after

surgery.

In

SR

terms,

Slow

Harm

can

accumulate

without

being

permanent.

This

distinction

introduces

recoverability

as

a

property

that

future

Slow

Harm

studies

should

estimate

rather

than

assume.

29.5 Death While Waiting Is Not the Same as Death Because of Waiting DWLᵢ = 𝟙 [death occurs before scheduled care] A death recorded while a patient is on a waiting list is an important access endpoint,

but

it

is

not

automatically

a

causal

estimate

of

mortality

attributable

to

delay.

DWL = 1 ⇏ Death caused by waiting

The causal quantity of interest is instead the counterfactual difference in mortality

under

alternative

waiting

exposures:

ATE  = E[M(EWT = e ₁ ) − M(EWT = e ₀ )] Estimating ATE  requires patient-level clinical severity, comorbidity, priority,

treatment

indication,

competing

risks,

and

timing.

Counts

of

people

who

die

before

receiving

care

can

establish

that

death-before-service

occurs;

they

cannot

determine

how

many

deaths

would

have

been

prevented

by

shorter

waits

without

a

causal

design.

29.6 The Queue as an Embodied Cost Transfer A queue is represented administratively as days or weeks, but the burden is experienced

by

the

patient.

During

the

interval

the

subject

may

absorb

pain,

restricted

mobility,

reduced

earnings,

sleep

disruption,

anxiety,

caregiver

dependence,

medication

use,

and

repeated

healthcare

contacts.

The

infrastructure

records

a

wait;

the

body

carries

the

interval.

Institutional Queue → Individual Exposure Time → Accumulated Burden

This produces a measurable form of Repair Burden Transfer. The system's capacity

constraint

is

partly

buffered

by

the

patient's

adaptation

until

treatment

occurs.

29.7 Identification Strategy for Waiting-Time Harm A basic patient-level model can begin with: Yᵢ = α + β EWTᵢ + γ ᵀXᵢ + δ   + δ   + ε ᵢ Yᵢ is a prespecified outcome; EWTᵢ is excess waiting; Xᵢ contains baseline severity,

age,

comorbidity,

socioeconomic

variables,

and

clinical

priority;

δ  

captures

hospital

or

regional

effects;

and

δ  

captures

calendar-time

effects.

The

coefficient

β

is

not

causal

unless

the

remaining

assignment

of

waiting

time

is

conditionally

exchangeable

or

a

stronger

design

supplies

identification.

Ontario data show substantial surgeon- and hospital-level variation in scheduled

non-urgent

surgical

waits,

which

creates

a

useful

research

setting

but

also

raises

selection

questions

(Sutherland

et

al.,

2024).

A

stronger

design

would

exploit

plausibly

exogenous

variation

in

capacity,

scheduling,

policy,

or queue assignment while testing whether that variation affects patient outcomes

through

waiting

time.

29.8 Falsification of the Waiting-Time Slow Harm Hypothesis The hypothesis is weakened if longer waiting, after credible adjustment or quasi-experimental

identification,

does

not

worsen

the

prespecified

intermediate

or

clinical

outcome;

if

deterioration

occurs

before

the

waiting

interval;

if

shorter

waits

fail

to

improve

outcomes

that

the

model

predicts

should

respond;

or

if

apparent

effects

are

fully

explained

by

clinical

priority

and

baseline

severity.

Waiting-Time Slow Harm is therefore not the claim that every wait is harmful.

It

is

the

claim

that

waiting

is

an

exposure

whose

health

cost

can

be

measured,

whose

effect

is

procedure-specific,

and

whose

causal

contribution

can

be

tested.

29.9 What This First Test Establishes — and What It Does Not The first test establishes operationality at a narrow level. A construct derived from

Infrastructural

Medicine

generated

a

directional

prediction;

that

prediction

could

be

mapped

onto

existing

clinical

measures;

quantitative

evidence could be evaluated against a null; and the evidence produced both support

and

a

boundary

condition.

This

is

sufficient

to

show

that

Waiting-Time

Slow

Harm

is

not

merely

rhetorical.

It

is

measurable

and

capable

of

being

constrained

by

data.

Waiting-Time Slow Harm: supported with boundaries The supported proposition is narrow: among patients awaiting elective primary

hip

or

knee

arthroplasty,

longer

presurgical

waiting

is

associated

with

greater

preoperative

functional

and

health-related

quality-of-life

burden

in

the

available

quantitative

literature.

The

current

evidence

does

not

establish

that

every

additional

day

produces

the

same

effect,

that

the

relationship

is

causal

in

every

dataset,

that

waiting

necessarily

worsens

postoperative

outcomes,

or

that

arthroplasty

findings

generalize

to

other

procedures.

The result should therefore be described as the first empirical support for one specified

prediction

of

Infrastructural

Medicine,

not

as

empirical

validation

of

the

entire

field.

Field-level

support

must

accumulate

through

independent

tests

across

mechanisms,

populations,

diseases,

infrastructures,

and

data-generating

processes.

29.10 Ten-Case Slow Harm Validation Program — Subsequent Work The next empirical phase is deliberately deferred. A ten-case program can test

whether

the

higher-order

Slow

Harm

architecture

recurs

across

domains

while

allowing

the

magnitude,

mediator,

latency,

and

recoverability

of

harm

to

differ.

Candidate

cases

include:

elective

arthroplasty

waiting;

cancer-treatment

waiting;

cardiac-intervention

waiting;

diagnostic-imaging

delay;

mental-health

service

delay;

food

insecurity

and

metabolic

burden;

housing

instability

and

health;

occupational

exposure;

pharmaceutical

shortage

or

interruption;

and

water

or

environmental

exposure.

These

cases

are

proposed

research

targets,

not

findings

of

this

first

edition.

The comparative object is not whether every case produces the same outcome.

It

is

whether

a

common

architecture

can

be

estimated

without

erasing

domain-specific

biology:

Infrastructure → Exposure / Constraint → Accumulation → Embodiment → Outcome

For waiting-time cases, future work should estimate at least two dimensions of

Slow

Harm:

accumulated

burden

A

and

recoverability

ρ.

A

useful

conceptual

representation

is:

SH = (A, ρ), 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 where ρ near 1 denotes a highly recoverable burden after effective intervention

and

ρ

near

0

denotes

largely

irreversible

loss.

No

numerical

value

of

ρ

is

assigned

in

this

manuscript.

Its

definition,

scale,

and

estimation

must

be

disease-

and

outcome-specific.

The

arthroplasty

evidence

only

motivates

the

distinction

because

measurable

preoperative

deterioration

can

coexist

with

an

absence

of

demonstrated

postoperative

penalty.

The ten-case program is intended to determine whether Slow Harm separates into

recoverable

waiting

harm,

progressive

waiting

harm,

event-risk

waiting

harm,

diagnostic

waiting

harm,

and

potentially

irreversible

forms.

Those

categories

remain

hypotheses

until

tested.

Their

inclusion

here

marks

the

field’s

empirical

agenda,

not

completion

of

that

agenda.

29.11 Sedation Loop — Subsequent Empirical Test Roadmap

The Sedation Loop can be tested with the same discipline. The primary empirical

challenge

is

to

operationalize

F,

L,

P,

D,

H,

and

K

independently.

A

valid

test

cannot

define

food-environment

pressure

from

the

same

disease

outcomes

the

model

is

trying

to

explain.

D  ₊₁ − D  = α F  − β L  + Γ ᵀC  + ε   H  = D  − γ P  K  ₊₁ − K  = δ H  + Ψ ᵀC  + υ   The strongest evidence for a sedation-like regime would combine persistent independently

measured

upstream

pressure,

increased

treatment

reach

or

effectiveness,

improvement

in

treatment-sensitive

outcomes,

and

continued

incident

or

residual

burden

that

is

not

explained

by

aging,

survival,

diagnostic

expansion,

or

compositional

change.

The

model

is

rejected

or

narrowed

where

those

joint

predictions

fail.

This manuscript therefore treats the existing Sedation Loop evidence as hypothesis-generating

and

specifies

the

next

empirical

step

rather

than

converting

proxy

trends

into

proof.

30. Empirical Program: Canada and Comparative Systems

The empirical program now begins from a demonstrated operational foothold rather

than

from

theory

alone.

Chapter

29

reports

a

narrow

secondary

quantitative

test

in

elective

hip

and

knee

arthroplasty

and

finds

support,

with

explicit

boundaries,

for

preoperative

Waiting-Time

Slow

Harm.

The

broader

program

below

remains

prospective.

It

is

designed

to

determine

whether

Slow

Harm,

the

Eroded

Subject,

the

Sedation

Loop,

and

related

infrastructural

pathways

reproduce

across

Canadian

and

comparative

systems.

28.1 Linked Data What matters for linked data is not its rhetorical plausibility but its measurable

position

in

the

pathway.

For

linked

data,

the

analysis

identifies

the

upstream

determinant,

the

exposed

population,

the

plausible

mediator,

and

the

downstream

outcome

that

should

change

if

the

proposed

pathway

is

real.

Separate

measures

are

required

at

each

stage

so

that

the

model

can

fail

at

a

specific

link

rather

than

survive

as

an

unfalsifiable

whole.

Causal interpretation improves when the model states what evidence would weaken

the

proposed

mechanism.

For

linked

data,

investigators

should

distinguish acute, repeated, intermittent, cumulative, and developmentally timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

This relationship is best evaluated as a sequence rather than as a static association.

In

models

of

linked

data,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

A longitudinal view is essential because the relevant load may precede diagnosis

by

years.

A

study

of

linked

data

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

28.2 Regional Variation Before regional variation can enter an SR model, its causal role has to be delimited.

A

study

of

regional

variation

should

trace

the

condition

from

its

upstream

source

through

exposure

and

mediation

to

an

outcome

that

can

be

observed

independently.

Each

component

should

be

measured

on

its

own

terms

so

that

a

broad

structural

category

cannot

stand

in

for

evidence.

Mechanistic plausibility is only the beginning; the proposed sequence must also

fit

observed

timing.

For

regional

variation,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

A stronger analysis begins by isolating the mechanism at issue. In models of regional

variation,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms therefore belong in the primary analysis rather than in a defensive

footnote.

For causal purposes, the decisive issue is how the proposed pathway behaves over

time.

A

study

of

regional

variation

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

28.3 Policy Changes The chapter treats policy changes as a candidate mechanism, not as a conclusion.

A

study

of

policy

changes

should

trace

the

condition

from

its

upstream

source

through

exposure

and

mediation

to

an

outcome

that

can

be

observed

independently.

Operational

independence

keeps

the

field

from

treating

the

existence

of

an

institution

or

environment

as

proof

of

its

biological

effect.

The relevant comparison is between plausible causal architectures, not between

structure

and

individual

agency.

For

policy

changes,

investigators

should distinguish acute, repeated, intermittent, cumulative, and developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

In

models

of

policy

changes,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

competing

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The next analytical step is to determine whether the observed pattern survives alternative

explanations.

A

study

of

policy

changes

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative record is not evidence of absence, but neither is incomplete measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

28.4 Natural Experiments Natural Experiments is the point at which the chapter becomes specific. A study

of

natural

experiments

should

trace

the

condition

from

its

upstream

source

through

exposure

and

mediation

to

an

outcome

that

can

be

observed

independently.

Those

quantities

should

enter

the

analysis

independently,

allowing

one

part

of

the

pathway

to

be

rejected

without

forcing

conclusions

about

the

rest.

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

For

natural

experiments,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

The empirical burden is therefore to identify what changed, when it changed, and

through

which

mechanism.

In

models

of

natural

experiments,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

and

factors

also

dominate

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection,

reverse

causation,

and

mechanisms

therefore

belong

in

the

rather

in

footnote.

empirically.

A

study

of

natural

experiments

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

Absence

28.5 Inequality For inequality, the causal claim begins with definition rather than interpretation.

Evidence

concerning

inequality

becomes

interpretable

when

the

proposed

driver,

exposure

pattern,

biological

or

behavioral

mediator,

and

expected endpoint are all measured. Each component should be measured on its

own

terms

so

that

a

broad

structural

category

cannot

stand

in

for

evidence.

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

For

inequality,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

A credible estimate must account for both the accumulation of load and the possibility

of

recovery.

In

models

of

inequality,

individual

action

may

mediate

rather

a

defensive

footnote.

Mechanistic plausibility is only the beginning; the proposed sequence must also

fit

observed

timing.

A

study

of

inequality

should

document

which

variables are directly observed, which are proxies, what is missing, and how recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

record

is

not

evidence

absence,

but

is

incomplete

evidence

at

easier

to

easier

should

distinguish

acute,

This chapter’s formalization are conceptual measurement scaffolds rather than

established

clinical

calculators.

They

state

what

would

need

to

be

if

to

28.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in linked data precede variation in the relevant health

outcome?

•

The empirical burden is therefore to identify what changed, when it changed,

and

through

which

mechanism.

•

This distinction matters because administrative records and causal processes do

not

necessarily

align.

•

The appropriate inference depends on the quality, timing, and granularity of the

available

evidence.

•

For policy changes, which populations or settings show the longest delay between

material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning natural experiments would falsify, reverse, or materially

narrow

the

proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

28.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on empirical program is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

31. Falsifiability and Failure Conditions Infrastructural Medicine must specify what would weaken it. If upstream infrastructure

measures

fail

to

predict

exposure;

if

exposure

does

not

precede

outcomes;

if

plausible

confounding

removes

associations;

if

interventions

changing

infrastructure

do

not

change

intermediate

or

health

outcomes;

or

if

effects

fail

to

replicate,

the

relevant

SR

proposition

must

be

narrowed

or

rejected.

29.1 Null Findings

The evidentiary problem posed by null findings is concrete. Research on null findings

should

distinguish

the

condition

producing

the

exposure

from

the

people

receiving

it,

the

mechanism

carrying

it

forward,

and

the

endpoint

used

to

test

the

claim.

Separate

measures

are

required

at

each

stage

so

that

the

model

can

fail

at

a

specific

link

rather

than

survive

as

an

unfalsifiable

whole.

Population-level regularities become useful only when they can be connected to

a

plausible

individual-level

pathway.

For

null

findings,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

Mechanistic plausibility is only the beginning; the proposed sequence must also

fit

observed

timing.

In

models

of

null

findings,

individual

action

may

mediate

an

upstream

condition,

modify

susceptibility,

respond

to

early

symptoms,

or

share

causes

with

the

outcome.

Biological

predisposition

and

protective

factors

can

also

dominate

in

particular

cases.

Confounding,

selection, reverse causation, and competing mechanisms therefore belong in the

primary

analysis

rather

than

in

a

defensive

footnote.

The model gains precision by separating what the system records from what the

subject

actually

experiences.

A

study

of

null

findings

should

document

which

variables

are

directly

observed,

which

are

proxies,

what

is

missing,

and

how

recognition

delay

could

bias

inference.

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

administrative

record

is

not

evidence

of

absence,

but

neither

is

incomplete

measurement

evidence

that

an

unobserved

structural

mechanism

must

exist.

29.2 Reverse Causation What matters for reverse causation is not its rhetorical plausibility but its measurable

position

in

the

pathway.

Evidence

concerning

reverse

causation

becomes

interpretable

when

the

proposed

driver,

exposure

pattern,

biological

or

behavioral

mediator,

and

expected

endpoint

are

all

measured.

Operational

independence

keeps

the

field

from

treating

the

existence

of

an

institution

or

environment

as

proof

of

its

biological

effect.

The relevant causal question changes once timing and exposure history are made

explicit.

For

reverse

causation,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated, intermittent, cumulative, and developmentally timed exposure where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

The model gains precision by separating what the system records from what the

subject

actually

In

Biological

Confounding,

A

which

which

are

proxies,

what

is

and

Absence

from

a

clinical

or

is

29.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on falsifiability is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

32. Governance and Prevention The policy implication of the field is not that every health problem requires maximal

state

intervention.

It

is

that

prevention

should

occur

at

the

level

where

causal

leverage

is

greatest.

Sometimes

that

is

individual

treatment;

sometimes product standards; sometimes water treatment; sometimes housing;

sometimes

labor

policy;

sometimes

clinical

redesign.

Evidence

determines

the

level.

30.1 Source Control Source Control cannot be inferred from the domain label alone. Research on source

control

should

distinguish

the

condition

producing

the

exposure

from

the

people

receiving

it,

the

mechanism

carrying

it

forward,

and

the

endpoint

used

to

test

the

claim.

No

single

composite

label

should

substitute

for

direct

evidence

about

the

source,

exposure,

mediator,

and

endpoint.

The observed outcome should be read alongside the conditions that made it more

or

less

likely.

For

source

control,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

30.2 Universal Design The evidentiary problem posed by universal design is concrete. The causal chain

for

universal

design

must

be

explicit

enough

to

show

where

the

pressure

originates,

who

bears

it,

how

it

is

embodied,

and

what

measurable

consequence

follows.

Each

component

should

be

measured

on

its

own

terms

so

that

a

broad

structural

category

cannot

stand

in

for

evidence.

At this stage, measurement has to distinguish the visible outcome from the underlying

process.

For

universal

design,

investigators

should

distinguish

acute,

repeated,

intermittent,

cumulative,

and

developmentally

timed

exposure

where

those

distinctions

are

relevant.

The

observation

window

must

also

include

plausible

latency

and

recovery;

otherwise

a

cross-sectional

snapshot

can

misrepresent

both

direction

and

magnitude.

This distinction matters because administrative records and causal processes do

not

necessarily

align.

models

universal

rather

in

footnote.

This question cannot be resolved from prevalence alone; it requires pathway-specific

A

universal

document

which

are

directly

which

are

proxies,

what

is

Absence

from

a

or

evidence

that

an

unobserved

clinical

30.8 Research Questions •

Does measurable variation in source control precede variation in the relevant

health

outcome?

•

The model gains precision by separating what the system records from what the

subject

actually

experiences.

•

The empirical burden is therefore to identify what changed, when it changed,

and

through

which

mechanism.

•

The observed outcome should be read alongside the conditions that made it more

or

less

likely.

•

For monitoring, which populations or settings show the longest delay between

material

change

and

reliable

recognition?

•

What result concerning accountability would falsify, reverse, or materially narrow

the

proposed

infrastructural

interpretation?

30.9 Chapter Conclusion The SR position on governance is therefore neither 'everything is infrastructure'

nor

'individual

choice

is

irrelevant.'

The

claim

is

that

infrastructure

is

a

causal

layer

that

can

be

specified,

measured,

compared

with

alternatives,

and

changed.

Where

evidence

supports

that

layer,

medicine

gains

an

upstream

target.

Where

evidence

does

not

support

it,

the

field

must

say

so.

33. Field Synthesis The unifying claim of Infrastructural Medicine is modest in wording but large in

consequence:

bodies

develop

inside

systems.

Medicine

already

knows

that

environment

matters.

Public

health

already

knows

that

social

conditions

matter.

SR's

to

formalize

infrastructure

as

the

transmission

architecture

connecting

policy,

markets,

material

systems,

exposure,

biology,

diagnosis,

and

repair.
